Suppose, for contradiction, that f*g is not primitive. Then there exists a non-unit element d in R that divides all coefficients of f*g. Since R is a UFD, d can be factored into irreducibles. Let me consider the coefficients of f and g. Since f is primitive, any common divisor of its coefficients must be a unit. Similarly for g. But if d divides all coefficients of f*g, then d must divide the coefficients of f or g, which would contradict their primitiveness. Wait, maybe I need to use Gauss's lemma here. Gauss's lemma states that the product of two primitive polynomials is primitive in a UFD. That's a key point.\n\nNow, for the second part: if f and g are coprime in R[x], they are coprime in F[x]. Coprimality in R[x] means there's no non-unit element in R that divides both f and g. In F[x], coprimality is defined by having no common non-constant polynomial factor. Since R is a UFD, and F is its field of fractions, any common factor in F[x] would correspond to a common factor in R[x], which contradicts their coprimality. Therefore, they must remain coprime in F[x].\n\nI should structure the f $ on that interval. Thus, the upper sum $ U(P, f) $ and lower sum $ L(P, f) $ are identical. Since the upper and lower integrals coincide, $ f $ is Riemann integrable.\n\n[[ ## completed ## ]]", StringSignature(query -> reasoning, response
